=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.97)
OEC28190
In reply refer to
Da 130-Pedley, Hilton S.
My dear Mr. Meisnere:
The receipt is acknowledged of your letter of December 9,
1948 in which you inquirs regarding the reasons which Mr. Hilton
894.
S. Pedley gave in support of his application for permission to
enter Japan. The Department understands that Mr. Pedley is a
British subject.
In this connection you are informed that permission to enter
Japan is granted only by the Suprene Commander for the Allied
Powers at Tokyo. In the case of an individual traveling in the
interest of an American firm, even though he is not an Ameriean
12-948
citisen, his application for permission to enter Japan is submitted
to the Supreme Commander at Tokyo by the Department of Commerce.
The function of the Department of State is merely to transmit to
the alien applicant the decision of the Supreme Commahder. In as
much as Mr. Pedley submitted to the Department of Commerce all the
information whioh he considered pertinent to his application, it
is suggested that you address your request for information eoncern-
ing Mr. Pedley to that Department. The Department of State has
observed, however, that on its application forms the Department
of Commerce makes the statement that information submitted thereon
is for the confidential use of the appropriate authorities.
Sincerely yours,
For the Secretary of State:
Walter F. Chappell
Assistant Chief
Division of Protective Services
3..22.
L CV.
Mr. Harry Meisnere,
2
51 East Forty-second Street,
New York 17, New York.

Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: December 14, 1948
28
FW 894.
SUBJECT:
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. Collender, of Paul E. Sernau,
294 Fifth Avenue, New York 10, New York.
Mrs. Mellor - DS.
COPIES TO:
2-1048
OPO
1-1493
Mrs. Mellor telephoned Paul E. Sernau to inform him that his
representative in Shanghai, Mr. Nafeh J. Sbath, had been granted
permission to enter Japan between December 15 and January 15 for
a stay of sixty days after entry. In the absence of Mr. Sernau
she talked with Mr. Collender, who asked her if Mr. Sbath's wife
had been granted permission to enter Japan. He was informed that
she had not been granted permission.
Mrs. Mellor asked if both addresses, 49 Szechuen Road and
P. O. Box 1059 should be mentioned in the telegram, in the interest
of lessening the cost of the cable, and he said it would be better
to use both addresses.
Mr. Collender authorized charging the cost of the cable to
Paul E. Sernau and thanked the Department for calling.

the proposed immigration to Japan of Ir. Paul Wolff von Plottegg
and his family.
ACTION
Mr. von Plottegg's aoplicution to enter Japan was referred
DOFIES
to the appropriate sections of General Headquarters, Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers, which has disapproved the applica-
TO:
CC
tion on the ground that individuals are not at present being admitted to
Japan for employment in rirms organized in Japan.
The Office of tne Civil Property Custodian of General Head-
quarters has in addition provided a summary (copy attached) of avail-
able derogatory informetion concerning the "Zewo" Manufacturing Works,
in Kobe, of which Ir. von Plotteg: desires to assune management. That
office, whose functions include custody of and jurisdiction over enemy
property in Japan, had previously nude a thorough investigation of
the tatus of the "sewe" ! anufacturing Works. As the attached summary
indicutes, the firm is considered te he German-organized, although at
tho mtd of the war ownership was nor inally transferred to Mrs. Gerda
von Wolff, a naturalized ur rien citizen and wife of one of the
original partners, in order to uveid the effeet of SCAP restrictions
on operation of Germun business coneerns in Jupan.
Although no adverse security information is available directly
concerning r. von Plottegg und his fumily, it is believed that the
unsavory reputation or r. Herbert von Wolff und the adverse informa-
tion regarding the "Zewo" Munufacturing Jorks would further preclude
entry cleurunce for Mr. von Plottegg. It may be stated in this
connection thet the allegations against Ir. and Mrs. von Wolff which
are set forth in p ragraph 2 of the Office of the Civil Property
Custodian's summary were widely current among foreign residents of
Kobe.
The provision of current policy of CHa, SCAP, which precludes
entry of individuals for employment in firms organized in Japan is
sufficient
1648

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.94)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 787,
December 16, 1948.
- 2 -
sufficient basis, at present, for not clearing Mr. von Plottegg
and his family to enter Jaoan. However, in view of the derogatory

ussuming management or the "zewo" Manufacturing Works even if

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Daopatch No. 787 dated
December 16, 1s8 from the United
States Political Adviser for Japan,
Tokyo, on the sucject "Application
RESTRICTED
For Entry into Japan, Paul Wolff
von Plottegg and Family".
SUMMARY OF INFORKATION FROM OFFICE OF THE
CIVIL PROPERTY CUSTCDIAN, GH, SCAP
subject:
Entry Clear nee for P.W. von Plottegg, Austrian National,
and Family
Date: 15 Oct 1948
1. Records on file with CPC indicate hat Zewo Manufacturing
Works was originated and operated by Herbe: von Wolff, former Nazi
Party member, and by Max enger, former At: he of German Embassy
in Tokyo, an "objectionable" Cerman natior, since repatriated.
A SCA directives precluded the operatior f Cerman business con-
cerns in Japan at that time, ewo Munufacturing Works was put in
the name of Mrs. Gerda von olff, allegedly a naturalized American
citizen end the wife of Herbert von Wolff. Mrs. von Wolff returned
to the United States shortly after the business was organized and
the management of the business was left to her husband. It is
therefore considered that her name as o'ner was used as a cloak to
hide the real owner of Jewo Manufacturing Works.
2. It is common knowledge among German nationals, acquaintances
of Mr. and Mirs. von olff, that Mr. von Wolff was expelled from the
Nazi Party in Japan because of notoriety attaching to his illegal
activities in foreign exchange for which he was arrested by the
Japanese uuthorities during the war; thut Mr. von Wolff subsequently
re-applied for admission to the Nazi Party, which was refused; that
during the war he supplied the Nazi U-boats and other German vessels
with critically needed medical supplies and instruments; that the
name "Zewo" Manufacturing Works was formed by the letters "ZE" from
Mr. enger's name and "Wo" from Mr. von Wolff's name; that Max
enger's ability as an engineer was used to aavantage in the business
formed by enger and von Wolff; that Max Zenger had charge of the
technical work in connection with the machinery used and the
manufactured products of this business; that Max Zenger was among
the first "objectionable" German nationals repatriated to Germany
in 1946; that presently Max Pergmann, an "objectionable" German
national is managing this business; that Mrs. von Wolff took no
active part in the said business, other than representing the firm
to the occupation authorities; that Mrs. von Wolff obtained and
attempted to obtain a loan or loans at Japanese banks upon false
representations as to guarantors of such loans or attempted loans
with which to pay the fine imposed on Mr. von Wolff for his illegal
foreign exchange activities; that during the war years Mrs. von
Wolff took full advantage of her original German nationality in
entertaining German U-boat officers and Nazi officials, as well
as Japanese officials.
3. In view of the above, it is reconmended that the application
of Mr. Paul Wolff von Plottegg be further investigated. CPC is
making further investigation regarding the present status of this
concern.
RESTRICTED

NO....19)
CONTROL OF ENTRY AND EXIT OF INDIVIDUALS,
AIRCRAFT AND SURFACE VESSELS INTO AND FROM JAPAN
Section
General....
Individuals.......
II
Aircraft.
III
Surface Vessels.
IV
I. GENERAL. 1. Rescission. Circular 8, General
Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers,
30 August 1946.
2. Purpose. To establish procedures to be followed
in tha control of individuals, aircraft and surface vessels
entering and leaving Japan, 1.e., the four main 1slands of
Hokkaido, Honshu, Shikoku and Kyushu and outlying 1slands
under control of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
3. a. Implementing Agencies.
(1)
The Commanding General, Eighth Army, will
control the entry into and exit from Japan
of the personnel menticned herein.
(2)
The Commander, Naval Activities Japan,
will control the entry and exit and the
movement of surface vessels while in
Japanese waters.
(3)
The Commanding General, Far East Air Forces,
will control the entry and exit and the
movement of aircraft into and out of Japan.
b. The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers will
keep the implementing agencies informed as to the action taken
on requests for entry into and departure from Japan of indi-
viduals, aircraft and surface vessels. This information will
include the period of validity of the permit.
c. The Commanding General, Eighth Army, will es-
tabliah customs, imigration and quarantine controls at ports
of entry and exit, both air and surface, utilizing such Jap-
anese agencies as are appropriate. These controls will
include:

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.99)
(C1r 19)
(1)
Medical examinations.
(2)
Required inoculations.
(3)
Customs inspection.
(4)
Maintenance of logistic facilities for
the detention of unauthorized personnel
arriving in Japan.
d. Implementing agencies shall have power of de-
tention over individuals, aircraft and surface vessels in
the event of failure to comply with this directive.
4. Ports of entry for aircraft and surface vessels will
be designated by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
at the time of approval for entry. Currently, the ports of
entry for both aircraft and surface vessels are:
a.
Aerial Ports:
(1)
Haneda Air Force Base, Tokyo.
(2)
Iwakuni Air Base, Iwakuni.
b.
Surface Ports:
(1)
Otaru
(2)
Hakodate
(3)
Yokohama
(4)
Yokosuka
(5)
Nagoya
(6)
Shimizu
(7)
Kobe
(8)
Kure
(9)
Sasebo
(10)
Miike
(11)
Nagasaki
(12)
Moji (closed for mine clearance).
5. The provisions of this circular do not apply to persons
being repatriated to and from Japan under the supervision of
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powere.
II.
INDIVIDUAIS. 6. Authorization to Enter Japan.
a.
Military, Naval, Air Force and civilian personnel
traveling on official orders for permanent duty with the U-
nited States Occupation Forces or for permanent or temporary
2

=== Page 91 === (confidence 0.98)
(C1: 19)
duty with the British Commonwealth Occupation Force: will
not be required to submit individual applications fo. entry
into Japan.
b. Dependents of personnel listed in paragraph ba
will be permitted entry into Japan in accordance with current
regulations for movement of dependents.
c. Individuals, other than those listed in paragraph
ba and b, desiring entry into Japan will obtain prior author-
1zation from the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
7. Requests for Authorization to Enter. a. Request for
authorization to enter Japan will be made to the Supreme Com-
mander for the Allied Powers by the diplomatic or equivalent
representative in Japan of the country whose national desires
to enter Japan. Applications for entry under the private
trade program may be made by the government of the nationality
elther of the firm or of the representative desiring entry.
In the event the country concerned does not have a representa-
tive in Japan, the request will be made through the mission
in Japan representing the interests of the country concerned,
or, if no such representation exists, through diplomatic
channels to the United States Department of State, which will
refer the request to the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers for consideration. Requests will be suhmitted in
sufficient time to reach the Supreme Commande- for the Allied
Powers at least ten days prior to the expecte.! ate of de-
parture for Japan.
b. Request for authorization to ent. Japan will
contain the following information:
(1)
Full name.
(2)
Place and date of birth.
(3)
Citizenship. If naturalized, date and
place of naturalization.
(4)
Present occupation, including name of
organization with which employed, if
applicable.
(5)
Full explanation of purpose of visit to
Japan and full description of nature of
work to be performed, including name of
organization with which applicant is to
be employed or attached or will repre-
sent, if applicable, and any title or
designation applicant will hold.
(6)
Particulars concerning subsistence and
housing arrangements in Japan.
(7)
Desired length of stay.
(6)
Desired date of entry.
3

=== Page 92 === (confidence 0.99)
(C1r 19)
(9)
Point of oripin cutside of Japan and final
destinaticn in Japan.
(1C)
Probable port of arrival in Japan.
(11)
Number, date of issuance, date of expiration
of passport and issuing authority. (Pass-
ports are not required of personnel travel-
ing in military uniform, who may instead
present official military travel orders.
If applicable, this should be so indicated.)
8. Action on Requests for Entry. a. Action on requests
for entry will be governed by tre following regulations:
(1)
Notification of action taken on the request
will be given by the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers through the same channels
as in pararraph 7a.
(2)
Approved requests for entry will constitute
a clearance by the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Fowers for entry into Japan.
Such clearance is valid for entry into
Japan for a period of six months from date
of issue, unless otherwise specified. In-
dividuals unable to arrive in Japan within
the six months period of validity must sub-
mit a new request.
9. Entry into Japan. a. Upon arrival at point of
entry each individual will:
(1)
Have in his possessicn a passport or
equivalent travel document (official
military travel orders in the case of
personnel traveling in uniform) together
with a statement in English from an auth-
orized representative of the country of
his nationality (in case of commercial
entrants, this statement may be from the
representative of his firm's nationality,
see paragraph 7a) at point of origin,
that he has been cleared by the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers for entry
into Japan; this statement will include
the information required by paragraph 7b.
(2)
Have in his possession a certificate
showing successful vaccination against
smallpox and typhoid fever within the
preceding twelve months. However, personnel
arrivinp from epidemic smallpox areas
may be required to show evidence of
a successful vaccination within the

(Cir 19)

without the required certificates will be
given the necessary vaccinations and placed
under observation or surveillance for a
sufficient period to determine their freedom
from these diseases.
(3)
Be subject to customs inspection. Customs
declarations will include all property and
all moneys, negotiable instruments, and
legal and commercial documents which the
individual wishes to take into Japan.

10. Exit from Japan. a. The following personnel will
not be required to submit individual applications for exit:
(1)
Military, Naval, Air Force, and civilian
personnel of the United States occupation
forces or British Commonwealth Occupation
Force departing Japan on official orders.
(2)
Personnel who enter Japan under the pro-
visions of the private trade program, and
who depart within sixty days of entry.
Passports of such personnel will be stamped
upon entry to indicate approval of exit
within sixty days of date of entry.
(3)
Personnel who have permission to enter
Japan for any reason for a stay of fifteen
days or less. Passports of such personnel
will be stamped upon entry to indicate
approval of exit within fifteen days from
date of entry or within a shorter period,
as applicable.
b. Individuals other than those listed in paragraph
l0a desiring to leave Japan will submit applications either
directly to the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers or
through their diplomatic or equivalent representative in
Japan in sufficient time to reach the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers at least ten days prior to the anticipated
date of departure from Japan. Applications for exit clear-
ance will be submitted on the attached form (inclosure 1),
in triplicate.
c. Upon approval of request for exit clearance,
the individual concerned will be notified to report to the
Diplomatic Section, General Headquarters, Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers, at Tokyo, or to the Yokohama or Kobe
Division of Diplomatic Section, to have the exit permit
placed in his passport.
d. At ports of exit, individuals will submit a
declaration of all property and a list of all moneys, negoti-
able instruments, and legal and commercial documents which
they intend to take with them from Japan.
e. On departure the passport or other equivalent
travel document wiil be stamped to show place and date of
departure. Baggage will be subject to customs inspection
upon departure.
1l. Exit from and Reentry into Japan. a. Individuals
desiring to depart and reenter Japan will submit application
on the attached form (inclosure 2) in the same manner as out-
lined in paragraph 10.
b. Reentry permits will be valid for not more than
six months from date of 1ssuance.
6

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.99)
(C1r 19)
c. Crew members of commercial aircraft permitted
to conduct operations through Japan may be granted permits
valid for repeated exit and reentry upon application of the
commercial airline concerned. Application should be submitted
in form similar to inclosure 2.
III. AIRCRAFT. 12. Control of Entry, Passage and Exit
of Aircraft into and from Japan. a. Aircraft, other than
those assigned to or serving the occupation forces will not
be permitted entry into, or passage over Japan unless auth-
orized by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
b. Aircraft flying into or out of Japan will
follow standard procedures established in Circular 4,
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers, 11 May 1948. For ports of entry see paragraph 4a.
c. Requests for authorization for aircraft passage
or entry will be submitted to the Supreme Commander for the
Allied powers through the same channels as prescribed in
paragraph 7a at least ten days prior to the date passage or
entry is desired, and will include the following information:
(1)
Number and type of aircraft.
(2)
Markings or identification radio call sign.
(3)
Duration and purpose of stopovers.
(4)
Purpose of flight.
(5)
Names and positions of crew members.
(6)
Name of English-speaking crew member (for
other than United States or British air-
craft).
(7)
Names of passengers, if any, and purpose
of their trip.
(8)
Description of cargo to be carried.
(9)
Details of route and timing of flight.
d. The representative of the requesting country
will be informed of the action taken on the request. Ap-
proved requests will be valid for five days from date for
which flight is approved. Any change in estimated time of
arrival will require twenty-four hours notice.
e. Each aircraft will have as a member of the
crew an English-speakinf radio operator.
f
Appropriate clearance will be obtained from the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers prior to departure
of the aircraft from Japan; no aircraft will take off from
any point in Japan unless its latest complete cargo manifest
7

=== Page 96 === (confidence 0.99)
(cir 19)
has been approved by the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers.
IV. SURFACE VESSELS. 13. Control of Entry and Exit
of Merchant Vessels. a. Merchant vessels other than those
assigned to or carrying cargo solely for the occupation
forces will not be permitted entry into Japan unless auth-
orized by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers. For
authorized ports of entry see paragraph 4b.
b. (1)
Applications for entry of merchant vessela
into Japanese ports will be submitted
directly to Civil Transportation Section,
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers, by the vessels'
agents in Japan. Bight copies of the
application will be provided. In in-
stances when a vessel requiring permission
to enter Japan does not have agency repre-
sentation in Japan at the time the appli-
cation is required, application for entry
may be submitted by the vessel owner through
the same channels as prescribed in para-
graph 7a. Each application for entry will
be submitted in sufficient time to reach
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
at least ten days prior to the date on which
the vessel is expected to arrive in Japan.
(2)
Applicants will be informed of action taken
on all requests. One copy of each approved
application for entry will be returned to
the applicant with a notation indicating
approval.
(3)
Approved applications for entry will be
valid for one month from the date for which
entry is authorized. Within this limitation,
however, any changes in vessels' arrival time
from those dates shown in applications for
entry should be given informally, if possible,
to Civil Transportation Section, General
Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers.
c. Applications for entry will contain the following
information:
(1)
Name of vessel.
(2)
Radio call sign of vessel.
(3)
Port into which entry is requested, and
the estimated date of vessel's arrival.

d. (1)
Bach vessel authorized to enter a Japanese
port will establish radio contact with
station wvvv at Yokohama, 120, 48 and 24
hours prior to its estimated time of ar-
rival at a Japanese port. At each of these
three times, the vessel will transmit a
message addressed to Commanding General,
Eighth United States Army, indicating the
port to which it is proceeding and latest
estimate of its arrival time. Any other
pertinent information may be included in
these messages.
(2)
Coastal radio station wVVV is operated by
the United States Army and handles official
messages pertaining to shipping on frequen-
c1es of 428, 4255, 8510, 12765 and 17020
kilocycles. Frequencies of 8510 and 12765
kilocycles are guarded continuously and may
be used for handling traffic. However, if
communication on these two frequencies is
difficult, ships will be requested to change
to 428, 4255 or 17020 kilocycles. The
frequency of 500 kilocycles 1s also puarded
continuously, but is limited to initial
contact and distress messages.
e. (1)
Upon the arrival of any vessel at a Japanese
port, the Master will provide the United
States Army authorities at the port with
two copies of the vessel's inward cargo
manifest showing all cargo to be discharged
from the vessel at the port. The Master
will also make the following documents
available for inspection by the United
States Army authorities at the port:
(a)
Vessol's certificate of registry.
(b)
Vessel's articles.
(c)
A list of the names of any crew members
and passengers who desire to go ashore.
(2)
United States Army authorities at each port
will provide the Master of each entering
vessel with a copy of the port regulations.
f. (1)
Any vessel which has been granted clearance
into a Japanese port will be permitted to
remain in that port a reasonable length of
time.
(2)
Crew members and passengers of any such
vessel will remain in the port until the
vessel denarts, unless they are otherwise
authorized to enter under the general

provisions for entry of individuals set

g. Prior to the departure of any vessel from a

(1) Deposit with the United States Army auth-

vessel's agent so deposits, one copy of

all cargo loaded into the vessel at the
port.
(2)
Obtain from the United States Army auth-
orities at the port an outward clearance,
indicating the vessel's next port of cali
and the route to be followed.
14. Control of Entry and Exit of Combatant Ships.
a. Combatant ships other than those assigned to
or supporting the occupation forces will not be permitted
entry into Japan unless author1zed by the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers.
b. Requests for authorization for entry of com-
batant ships will be submitted to the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers through the same channels as prescribed
in paragraph 7a, and will contain the following information:
(1) Name and type of vessel, giving length,
beam and draft.
(2)
Radio call sign of vessel.
(3)
Number of officers and men in the crew.
(4)
Ports to be visited with estimated date
of arrival and departure from each port.
(5)
Name and rank of Commanding Officer.
(6)
Name and rank of Flag or General Officer,
or very important persons on board as
passengers.
(7)
Purpose of visit.
(8)
Brief statements of any special facilities
required.
(9)
Whether vessels have current information
on mine-fields in Japan. Course to bo
followed in Japanese waters.
(10)
Complete description of all cargo carried,
which is to be unloaded in Japan.
11

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.98)
(C1r 19)
c. While in Japanese waters, combatant ships will
report to the Commander, Naval Activities Japan, for oper-
ational control for navigational purposes.
d. A complete list of passengers for Japan with
identifying particulars will be presented with the manifest
to the port of disembarkation.
e. Crew and passengers for Japan will be cleared
for entry in accordance with the provisions for entry of
individuals in section II.
f. A complete description of all cargo to be
loaded at any point in Japan will be presented to the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers for prior approval.

RANGOON
Sir,
PROCUREMENT OF A MILITARY PERMIT TO ENTER JAPAN
I have the honour to state that I desire to go to
Japan for trade contacts and have been experiencing
a great difficulty to get a Military Permit to enter
Japan.
It may be mentioned that I have approached on the advic
of the Foreign Office the American Embassy for necessary
action, but they asked me to go and see the British
Embassy who were said to be looking after the interests
of Burmese citizens in Japan. The British Embassy said
in turn that they were not in a position to override
the Consular regulations observed between them and the
Burma Foreign Office, whichn was advised to take initiativ
in the matter.
I also sent a cable to the Burmese Embassy in Washingtoi
and their reply and a copy of my cable is attached
herewith for favour of information.
As I desire to leave for Japan about the 22nd of
December 1948, I shall be grateful if you eill kindly
cable to Burmese Enbassy in Wa shington to get necessary
permit before that date.
Yours faithfully,
For BA HAN & COMAPNY
/s/ BA HAN
PROPRIETOR

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.97)
A1
8.18
14. Hr. Gesa Vajda 1s owner of the firm of G. Vajda & Co., 320 Ssechuen Read,
Shanghai, China. His home address is: 147 Kangping Lu (Magniny), Shanghai, ft.
China. He is a Csech citisen.
Mr. Vajda has been, since the end of the war, our agent for China purchasing
for us materials worth several m1llion dollars a year. On December 16, 1947
this firm and Mr. Vajda made an agreement according to which Mr. Vajda would
be in charge of all our purchasing and selling activities in the Fer East.
His territory includes Japan, Korea, China proper, Hong Kong, Siam and Burma.
Implementing this agreement, it was decided to send Mr. Eudolf Riess to Tokyo
as resident agent for this firm. Mr. Riess is working, however, under the
direet supervision of Mr. Vajda.
Following the considerable expansion of our activities in Japan during 194s,
details of which are given below. it has been decided that Mr. Max M. Teissh,
Assistant Secretary of our Company, should visit Japan in order to check on
business being done at present and enlarge business connections for a further
expansion in l949. Mr. Teisch applied for permission to visit Japan on
November 17th. It is very desirable from our point of view that Mr. Vajda.
who has an intimate knowledge of conditions in the Far East, should be in Japan
at the same time that Mr. Teisch is visiting that country in order to
introduce Mr. Teisch to both Japanese business connections and Occupation
Authorities there. It is also felt that it is desirable that Mr. Vajda should
accompany Mr. Teisch since Mr. Teisch plans to visit several Works outside of
Tokyo and Mr. Riess, owing to pressure of business, will not be in a position
to leave Tokyo for any length of time.
Some of our recent activities in Japan have included the following:-
We were able to obtain for Boeki-Cho Virgin Pig Lead, a very scarce
commodity urgently needed in Japan, at the rate of approximately $600,000.00
per month. All orders given to us by Boeki-Cho for this material have been
completed or are in the process of shipment.
We have purchased from Boeki-Cho surplus Zinc valued at $500,000.00.
We have purchased from Doeki-Cho Ferro Alloys, both surplus and current
production, valued at several hundred thousand dollars.
We have just been awarded a contract for Zinc Concentrates produced in
Japan valued at over $300,000.00.
We feel that with the presence of Mr. Teisch and Mr. Vajda in Japan, we can add
considerably to the business being done at present. We are especially interested
in surveying available supplies of Scrap Metals in Japan which are urgently
needed in this country.
Under the ciroumstances it is hoped that permission will be given for both
Mr. Vajda and Mr. Teisch to proceed to Japan early in 1949.

1949
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
The Embasey of razil presents its compliments
to the De artment of State and, with reference to the
note verbale of November 17, 1948, concerning the return
of Yosuitaro Suzuki to Brazil, has the honor to inform it
of the following:
2.
In view of the requirements which may be made
for the granting of exit permits, the Department of
State sug ests, in its memorandum of November 13, 1948,
that the Brazilian Government transmit to the Legation of
Portugal in Tokyo together with the necessary euthorization
for the granting of a permanent visa, a Brazilian passport
for foreigners, a certificate of identification, or a
similar locument, on which
the military euthority may
place an exit visa.
3.
In reply, the Ministry of Foreign Relations communi-
cates to the Embassy that the suggestion of the Department
of Sta e cannot be accepted, since passports for foreigners
are granted only to stateless persons who desire to leave
Brazilian territory; nor is it ineumbent on the Brazilian
authority to furnish a certificate of identification or
a similar document to foreigners who are not in Brazil.
4。
Only the)oalitaryudccupatioh authorities may
furhish
5
Dist

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.97)
一 2 -
furnish Mr. Suzuki with the necessary documents to leave
Japanese territory. However, in the above-mentioned
memorandum the Department of State refers to the regulation
which expressly forbids the said authorities to issue
such documents.
5.
Under such circunetances the Embassy requests
that the Department of State use its good offices with the
Supreme Command of the Allied Forces in Japan to the end
that an exception to the established rule may be made in
the case of M. Suzuki. Otherwise he has no alternative
except to abandon completely the hope of being permitted
to rejoin his wife, a Brazilian citizen, and their child-
ren, also Brazilians, residing and domiciled in Brazil.
Vashington, January 13, 1949.

Gentl emen:
W e have received an incuiry from mr. Jonm Zie ler of this city
regarding a business trip to Japan by air early In Merch, 1949.
Mr. Ziegler 1s a U.S.citizen and he wants to have a business associate
accompany him.
The business associate is a citizen of Brazil, a Mr. Theophilo
Badin, address 5l Sacudura Cabral, Rio de Janeiro. This gentleman
represents Mr. Ziegler's firm in Rio, the firm beine a U.S. firm
doing business as Crystal Products Co., Inc, incorporated in Missouri.
-1949
Mr. 7i egler hopes to have Mr. Badin come to the United States from
Rio on one of routine business visits and while here go to Tokyo and
on around the world in six weeks to two months time.
The gentleman in Rio is a very heavy stockholder in this company.
In Rio he handles the purchase, etc of many tons of quartz crystal. The
purpose of their trip would be to sell to the Japanese outlet excess
supplies which are now on hand. One man cannot go without the other.
It takes both of them to do the job. In the event they do not find
a market for their product in Japan they would probably exit to the
Phillipines, possibly going from there to Siam on thru India to Cairo
working their way back home.
We would appreciate very much your kind advice as to whether
you think this matter could be coordinated to bring about the necessary
approvals to enter Japan by the interested parties, under the conditions
described above.

The Consul in charge has the honor to refer to the
Department's unnumbered instruction, dated September 27,

1948, in which this office was requested to deliver a
Cuban passport properly completed to Mr. Macoto Yunaka

894.
In accordance with the Department's instructions
Mr. Sasaki was contacted and his exit clearance from

Subject: Revision of SCAP Regulations Concerning Private
Comu.ercial Entrants in Japan.
894
The Acting Political Adviser for Japan has the honor to
refer to this Mission's despatch No. 329 dated June 7, 1948,
Subject: "Forwarding Guide for Commercial Entrants to Japan"
and to forward for the information of the Department General
Headquarters, Supreme Conmander for the Allied Powers Circular
No. 1 of January 14, 1949, subject, "Private Comercial Entrants".
This circular replaces GHQ, SCAP Circular No. 3 of February 25,
1948, which was forwarded to the Department with this Mission's
despatch under reference, as amended in minor points by SCAP
10
Circulars No. 20 of June 24, 1948, and No. 25 of July 27, 1948.
14
The only substantial point in which the new cireular differs
6
from Circular 3 or 1948 as amended is the addition of paragraph
II.2.e. to the list of purposes for which entry of commercial
entrants is permitted. The addition,
"Conducting business and investment activities in
conformity with applicable regulations of the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers and Japanese
law"
is based upon this Headquarters' recent authcrization of limited
Foreign investment in Japan.
It may be mentioned that further suggested revisions of
SCAP regulations concerning commercial representatives in Japan
have ccme to the attention of this Mission subsequent to the
publication of Circular 1. Proposed changes would serve chiefly
to reduce the limitations on travel of commerciel entrants within
Japan, and to liberalize the restrictions on the number of
representatives from a single firm permitied entry at one time
and on dependents accompanying commercial entrants.

II. GENERAL.
2. Upon properly sponsored application, approved as hereinafter
provided, ontry of private commercial entrants to Japan is permitted
provided their entrance is for one or more of the following purposes:
a. Engaging in private trade for the purchase of, or to make
arrangements for future purchasos of potential exports, or to sell or
make available the raw materials or other commodities which Japan must

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.99)
(Cir 1)
import in ordor to increase the volume of foreign trade.
b. Providing services necessary and attendant to international
trade.
c. Seeking restitution of holdings in Japan prior to the war,
the restitution of which would contribute to the economic rehabilitation
of Japan.
d. Investigating business and investment possibilities which
would contribute to the economic rehabilitation of Japan.
e. Conducting business and investment activities in conformity
with applicable regulations of the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers and Japanese law.
3. The provisions of this circular do not operate in any way to
discontinue the authorization for trade representatives and commercial
missions for the conduct of international trade on a government-to-
government basis. (Memorandum from General Headquarters, Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers, for Chiefs of Allied Missions in Japan, AG O9l.31
(1 May 47) ESS/EX, subject: Trade Representatives in Japan, 4 May 1947).
IIL. APPLICATION FR ENTRY.

Applications should be submitted to the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers by the representative in Japan of the country of the national de-
siring, entry. In the event that the country concerned does not have a
diplomatic representative in Japan, the request may be made through diplo-
matic channels to the United States Department of State, or through tho
diplomatic mission in Japan representing the country's interest.
b. Applications will contain the following information:

Full name.

including name of organization with which applicant
is to be employed, attached or will represent.
(6)

are available for not to exceed 60 days.)
(7)
Desired length of stay.

(11)
lumber, date of issuance, period of validity of
passport and issuing authority.
c. Applications will be considered by the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers in priority of their receipt. Entry permits will
be valid during a period of 60 days.
d. Not more than two representatives from any single private
firm or enterprise (other than those companies providing services at-
tendant with trade) will be permitted entry at any one time when such
entry requires use of Japanese Government operated hotels.
e. Upon arrival of a commercial entrant at point of entry:
travel document, together with a statement in English

for entry into Japan; this statement will include the

Allied Powers. The continued presence of a commercial entrant in Japan
is contingent upan his contributing to the achievement of the objectives
of the occupation. Actions which are inimical to the objectives of the
occupation, or which operate to the detriment of the economic rehabili-
tation of Japan, are cause for revocation of entry permits.
IV. STATUS OF COMMERCIAL ENTRANTS.
7. A commercial entrant who is authorized to enter Japan under
the provisions of this circular is not a member of the occupation forces
or attached to or accompanying such forces. Such entrant is subject
not only to all laws and regulations specifically made applicable by
their terms to commercial entrants, but also to all laws and regulations
applicable in Japan to other nationals of his country who are not members
of, or attached to, or accompanying the occupation forces, whether such
laws or regulations are promulgated by the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers or pursuant to authority delegated by him, or by the
Japanese Govemment.
8. All commercial entrants in Japan are prohibited from committing
any act prejudicial to the securi ty of the occupation forces or any
member thereof, or any persons attached to or accompanying the occupation
forces. Conmercial entrants are subject to arrest by military authorities
for the violation of applicable regulations of the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers, including but not limited to the following prohibi-
tins:
a. Entering any of the following areas and installations:
(1) Temporary enclosures and stockades for war criminals.
(2)
Dump areas containing confiscated war materials and
supplies.
5

=== Page 216 === (confidence 0.98)
(Cir 1)
transaction of authorized business.
(4)
All other areas and installations that have been
placed "off limits" for security purposes.
b. Carrying arms or other lethal weapons.
c. Failing to comply with travel regulations promulgated by
the occupation forces.
9. a. A commercial entrant is subject to arrest for offenses
committed in Japan against the laws and regulations mentioned in para-
graph 7, or for violation of the provisions of tlis circular. Such
arrest in the case of a United Nations national will be made by the
military police, except that in areas where troops of the occupation
forces are not actually present on duty, and there is reasonable evi-
dence that a serious offense has been committed, or when otherwise
ordered by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers or his authorized
subordinates, arrest may be made by the Japanese police. Arrest in the
case of persons not United lations nationals may be made by either the
military or the Japanese police.
b. Trial for offenses committed in Japan, where the accused
is a United Hations national, will be by military court. In a case
where the accused is not a United  ations national, in the absence of
specific direction by competent authority tlat the trial be by military
court, trial may be either by a Japanese criminal court or by military
court.
10. All persons or organizations in Japan not attached to or accompa-
nying the occupation forces are subject to Japanese civil law and are
subject to the jurisdiction of Japanese courts in civil matters, except
as modified by applicable directives of the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers. The Japanese Jovemment has been prohibited from im-
posing discriminatory taxes against non-Japanese nationals and from im-
posing any tax based upon, or measured in, non-yen currencies. United
Nations nationals are also exempt fram any tax against them or their
property designed primarily to meet reparations or other charges in-
curred by the Japanese Govemment because of World Nar II, such as the
Capital Levy, the Non-War Sufferers pecial Tax, and part of the War
Indemmity Special Measures Law. Except as indicated above, yen income
is subject to Japanese taxation, and the Japanese Government has the
power to carry out property attachment proceedings and other law en-
forcement actions of a noncriminal nature. liowever, decisions in all
civil cases affecting Unitei Nations nationals or organizations, or
in which such nationals or organizations are or may become parties,
shall be subject to review, including revision or such other action as
may be considered necessury by the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers or his authorized representatives.
1l. Commercial entrants will conduct their business and investment
activities, including international trade, in accordance with provisions
of Circular 2, General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers, 14 January 1949, and applicable regulations. With the exception
of items for personal use and such commorcial items as may be necessary
and appropriate in the conduct of authorized business, (see paragraph
4e(2)), all international trade will be conducted subject to approval
6

=== Page 217 === (confidence 0.98)
(Cir 1)
by the Japanese Board of Trade (Boeki Cho). Although negotiations with
individual Japanese are prmitted, private contracts and agreements are
not valid unless approved and validated by the appropriate governmental
authority.
12. Commercial transactions with occupation force agencies or
personnel are not permitted without specific written authority.
13. Except as specifically permitted by license or other written
authority, commercial entrants are not authorized to use or possess
military payment certificates.
11. Financial transactions will be conducted in accordance with ex-
change controls and other applicable occupation force and Japanese Govern-
ment regulations. All foreim currencies, including United Statesdollars,
will be converted or deposited within 43 hours after arrival. Use of such
currencies in Japan is illegal except that durin: the first 48 hours after
arrivil commercial entrants may purchase foreim trade payment certificates
(in amounts necessary to meet personal expenses)and Japanese yen. There-
after purchases of foreim trade payment certificates and yen may be made
with acceptable foreign exchange instruments. In all cases except in the
course of business activities specifically permitted by the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers, conversion of foreign currency intc yen
throu;h other than authorized ofricial sources and at official rates is
prohibited.

15. a. Commercial entrants will receive those logistic privileges
and use of facilities of the occupation forces which are herein author-
.
b. The Japanese overnment has been charged with the respon-

entry when such entry will require the use of Japanese commercial hotel
facilities, facilities requisitioned or procured by the occupation forces,
or support from the occupation forces.
21. Automobiles may be brought to Japan only after a semipermanent
resident's permit has been obtained.
VI. FUNCTICNS OF GENERAL HEADQUARTERS, SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE
ALLIED POWERS.
22. General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers,
will perform the following functions:
a. Issue final approval for entry into Japan, informing the
Commanding General, Eighth Army, of each clearance granted.
b. Establish procedures for:
(1) Licensing, pricing and payment in international trade
transactions including validation of import and ex-
port licenses and approval of contracts.
(2)
Licensing persons and firms engaged in business in-
volving the receipt of foreign exchange in Japan,
such as banking, shipping, insurance, etc., or the

regulations.

(1) Briefing as to the regulations goveming the personal
conduct of the entrants, and the consumption of their
commercial business.
(2)
Supervision over the accomodations furnished by the
Japanese Government.
(3)
Facilities for deposit of all foreign currencies and
for the exchange of convertible currency for foreign
trade payment certificates and Japanese yen.
(4)
Issuance of travel orders required by paragraph 4f.
(5)
Issuance of statement of clearance which the commeroial
entrant will present to a representative of Commanding
General, Righth Army, at port of exit.
d. Maintain necessary records as to mumber of commercial en-
trants entering and leaving Japan and submit necessary reports to the
Department of the Army.
VII. RESPONSIBILITIES OF COMANDING GENERAL, EIGHTH ARMY.
23. The Commanding General, Eighth Army, has the same responsibility
for entry and exit of commercial entrants as for other categories of in-
dividuals and will perform the following additional functions:
a. Receive commercial entrants at ports of entry and super-
vise necessary customs inspection to prevent the importation of items
prohibited by current directives.
b. Register commercial entrants immediately upon entrance.
c. Check military permit, passport, and authority to enter
against list furnished by the Supreme Commander for the Alliod Powers.
d. Chock medical records and provide necessary imminization
and vaccination (requirements are the same as for occupation personnel).
e. Coordinate with the Japanese Government to arrange for the
prompt movement of the individual.
f. Provide emergency attention. Except in case of emergency
or when enroute or occupation force trains, occupation force rations
and billets will not be provided.
g. Provide emergency medical treatment at military medical
facilities to the extent of availability and limited to that required
9

=== Page 220 === (confidence 0.99)
(Cir 1)
to prevent undue suffering or to save life or limb.' Elective medical
and dental care will not be provided. Medical services provided will
be on a reimbursement basis at the dollar rates prescribed in current
publications.
h. Keep necessary records and notify General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, whenever a commercial entrant
acheduled to depart from Japan fails to depart.
i. At port of exit, verify clearance issusd by General Head-
quarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, and collect identi-
fication cards.
VIII. MISCELIANEOUS.
24. Japanese government commercial hotels are primarily established
and operated for the use of commercial entrants. Permission to engage
in intemational trade, or other business or investment activities granted
by or in accordance with Circular 2, General Headquarters, Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers, 1h Jamuary l949, does not entitle other personnel,
including non-Japanese.who have been continuously residents in Japan since
2 September 1945 or who have been permitted entry by the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers for the purpose of establishing permanent residence,
to the use of Japanese government facilities operated primarily for com-
mercial entrants, or to the privileges and use of occupation facilities
herein authorised for commercial entrants.
25. No member of the ocoupation forces and no person attached to or
accomparying the occupation forces, including his dependents may partici-
pate in intermational trade or other business or investment activity in
Japan for personal gain or advantage, or on behalf of any private person
or agency, except as specifically licensed by General Headquarters, Su-
preme Commander for the Allied Powers.
26. With the exceptions noted above commercial entrants may desig-
nate Japanese, Japanese firms, or foreign nationals in Japan as their
agents, subject to approval of the Japanese Government and the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers.
27. Commercial entrants may utilize to the extent of availability
the International Hospitals located at Tokyo, Yokohama, Kobe and Osaka.
Payment for services received will be made in Japanese yen.
28. Religious services available to the cccupation forces will be
available to cammercial entrants.
29. Amusements: a. Concerts and similar entertainment and sports
contests for occupation forces where no admission charges are made will
be available to commercial entrants as spectators.
b. Entertainment where fees are charged to occupation forces
will be available accordingto contract agreements of the entertainment
concemed.

(C1r 1)
c. Private blub privileges will be extended according to

The Secretary of State presents his compliments to His Exeelleney
the Ambassador of Burma and has the honor to refer to the Embassy's
note of January 6, 1949 under cover of which there was enelossd a
copy of a letter from the Foreign Office at Rangoon tranamitting the
application of U Ba Han for permission to enter Japan.
Mr. Han's application was presented to the appropriate military
authorities and in reply the Department received a communication
2849
stating that in accordance with Ciroular eight of the Supreme Com-
mander for the Allied Powers at Tokyo Mr. Han's application should be
presented through British channels in view of the fact that Burmese
interests in Japan are under the protection of the United Kingdom.
CS/JEC
In the interest of saving time, since Mr. Han expressed the desire
to enter Japan before the Burmese Government Trade Delegation
departed, the Department requested the United States Political Adviser
at Tokyo to present Mr. Han's application to the Supreme Commander.
The Department has now been advised that Mr. Han received Theater
02
clearance
22.22s
CE
THat.

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.95)
-2
clearance to enter Japan between January 27 and Mareh 27, 1949, for
e stey of sixty days after his date of entry. Notifieation of
Mr. Han's clearance was sent to the United Kingdom Liaison Mission
on January 27, 1949 for transmittal to Mr. Han through the appro-
priate authorities at Rangoon.
The United Kingdom Liaison Mission at Tokyo has been requested
to invite the attention of appropriate British representatives in
Burma to the Supreme Commander's directive which provides that
applications for entry of non-Americans into Japan should be sent
through diplomatic channels to the Department of State only if there
is no diplomatic mission in Japan representing or protecting the
interests of the country concerned. In view of the fact that the
interests of Burma in Japan are under the protection of the United
Kingdom, applications of this kind will presumably be presented
through British channels in the future.
Department of State,
Washington,
February 11, 1949.

other than members of the Occupation Forces may carry on entering

or leaving Japan. Also enclosed is a copy of a press release dated
January 25, 1949, issued by this Headquarters in explanation of the
revised rules.
The revised rules authorise all foreign nationals in Japan eli-
gible for repatriation to their homelands unaccompanied and license-
free shipmont of all personal effects and in addition professional
instruments and tools of trade in amounts not to exceed 4,000 pounds.
Japanese yen currency can not be taken from or brought into Japam but
will be taken up and held for the account of individuals surremdering
it (except for special arrangements under which persons being repe-
6
triated to Korea and the Ryukyu Islands will be enabled to receive
stated equivalents upon arrival in their home country). All persons
entering Japan must exchange foreign currency or turn it over for
cus tody.
The relaxation of rules in regard to Koreans was made at the
instance of this Mission in order to facilitate the repatriation
to Korea of Koreans in Japan.

Enclosure No. 2 to Despatch No. 64
dated January 31, 1949 from the United
States Political Adviser for Japan,
Tokyo, on the subject "Customs Controls
on Property Brought in or Taken out of
UNCLASSIFIED
Jaoan".
( C O P Y )
Press Release:
25 January 1949
REVISICN OF CUSTOMS CO.. TROLS DIRECTED BY SCAP
Revision of controls governing movement of property ineluding
currency and financial instruments which individuals, other than mem-
bers of the Occupation Forces, may carry on entering and leaving Ja-
pan has just been ordered by a SCAP directive, it was stated today
by a spokesman for SCAP's Economic and Scientific Section.
The new directive affords more liberal treatment to Chineg,
Ayukyuans and Koreans, resident in Japan and eligible for repatri-
ation to their respective homelands.
The CaP directive authorizes all foreign nationals in Japan
eli ible for repetriation to their homelands, unaccompanied and li-
cense-free shioment of ell personal effects and in addition profes-
sional instruments and tools of trade in amounts not to exceed 4,000
pounds. SCAP valid:ted license will be required only in instances
where such instruments and tools of trade exceed this limitation.
Japanese yen currency will not be permitted entry into or egress
from Japan. Yen taken up from persons entering and leaving Japan will
be held to the account of ti.e individual surrendering the currency
pending further negotietions. However, an agreement hes been reached
with the government of the Republic of Korea permitting Korean repa-
triates on their departure from Japan to exchange yen in amounts not
to exceed loo, oon yen per hend of e femily for a receipt negotiable
in Korea for Korean won. A similar arrangement has been erranged
for all Ryukyuen renatriates with the same 100,O00 yen limitation.
Another departure fron previously existing regulations is
that under foreign exchange controls incorporated in the new direc-
tive all persons entering Japan will be required to surrender foreign
currency at debarkation ports. Persons heving United States dollar
currencies will be permitted to exchange such currencies for yen at
the militery conversion rate or to deposit their United States curren-
cy in one of the foreign benks licensed to maintain a branch office
in Japan. All other foreign currencies and United States currencies
not exchanged for Japanese yen or denosited in a foreign bank will
be held in safe-ke ping at the Bank of Japan to the account of the
individual surrend ing the currency for return to him on his de-
parture from Japan.
United States doliar currencies deposited in a SCAP licensed
bank may be available in accordance with SCAF's foreign exchange
controls. Financial instruments expressed in foreign currencies
such as checks, drafts and money orders are not restricted under
the new directive, except as held by Japanese nationals entering
Janan.
Persons leaving Japen will be permitted to take only such for-
eign currencies as they surrendered for safe-keening on entering
Jadan
UNCLASSIFIED

Jepanese nationals permanently domiciled abrcad entering Japan
under SCAP's vritten authorit: as commer cial representatives, or in
any other canacity for a temoorrry visit will be subject to the
same customs cleerance as foreign ationels entering Japan.
However, Jepanere nationrls returning to Japan as repatriates
will be permitted to take into Japan ell ersonal possessions. They
will be required to surrender financial instruments expressed in for-
eign currencies which will be turned over to the Bank of Japan for ne-
gotiation. Upon necotiation, proceeds will be turned over to the per-
son who surrendered the currency. Foreign currencies will be taken
up from such individurls nd other than United States dollars held
in the Bank of Jepan until such time a currency surrendered can be
exchanged for Japanese yen. United States dollar currencies will
be exchanged at the port of entry for Japanese yen at the prevailing
military exchange rate.

FOREWARD
ielcome to Japen!
You are coming to a country where the effects of los-
ing a wer are still apparent in many ways in elmost evory
city. You may well find present-day Japan different from
anything you have experienced tefore, and the differences
will have a considerable influence on your doing business
here.
You will find thet Japan, under the supervision of
the Allied Occupetlon Forces, Is stead!ly progressing
toward the realization of a sound, peaceful, and self-
sustaining economy. The Japanese themselves, as well as
the Occupation authorities, recognize the great importance
of foreign trace in achieving this aim. Both the Occupa-
tion Forces and the Japanese will be ready to cooperate
with you and sssist you, in all appropriate ways, when
you come to Japan.
It is probatly unnecessery to tell you that a variety
of problems will confront you in doing business in an
occupied country, tut you will find many opportunitias for
business which will offer sutstantial advantages to you
and to your country and which will, in addition, contribute
to the reestatlishment of Japan as a responsible member
of the family of nations.
This booklet has been compiled to assist you in your
business activities by describing the current restrictions
and controls.
1

=== Page 282 === (confidence 0.99)
ENTRY
At all ports of entry you will be cleared through
Japanese Customs in accordance with current directives of
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers end will fill
out a "Customs Declaration Form" covering your personal
property inclusive of any shipped separately.
You mey tring into Japan without special license only
property intended for your personal use and tona fide sam-
ples or osher commercial items of a value not exceeding
500 which may te necessary and appropriate in the conduct
of your authorized business. You will not te authorized
to sell such items to the Japenese.
Any additional property or cargo imported into Japan
will require an import license, including samples in com-
mercial quantities not consigned to the Japanese Poard of
Trade and other commercial items necessary in the conduct
of your authorized operations. Automobiles may not be
brought into Japan until after you have obtained a "semi-
permanent" resident's permit.
While you are being cleared for customs, necessary
verification of your clearance and registration will be
accomplished, and you will be provided with an identifica-
tion card which vou will be required to carry at all times
during your stay. in Japan.
Upon completion of these steps you will be assigned
to one of the hotels operated by the Japanese Government
for your convenience in Tokyo, Nagoya, Osaka and Kyoto.
Travel to the hotel will be arranged and necessary tickets,
together with directions, will be provided. Every effort
will be made to permit you a choice of your hotel locetion,
rut because of the limited fecilities currently available,
assignments will have to be made on a "first-come, first-
served" tasis, and single rooms are extremely limited in
number.
If the vessel on which you are & passenger should he
diverted to a port other than Yokohama or Kobe, you should
proceed to the Military Government Headquarters located at
that port where necessary errangements will be made to in-
sure your travel to the proper centers for registration
at Kobe or Tokyo.
EXTENSION OF STAY
Your original clearance authorizes you to stay in Japan
for 60 days. Should you desire to stay beyond 60 days you
may apply after arrival for a "semi-permanent".resident's
permit. Application forms are evailable in the offices of
the Trade Services Division, Hotel Tokyo. Your application
should be submitted as early as practicable in order that
it may receive necessary consiceration.

=== Page 283 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTURE AND REENTRY
Individuals desiring to depart and reenter Japan should
submit applicetions for reentry permits, which are valid
for not more than six months from the date of issuance, at
lesst 10 deys prior to anticipated date of departure.
EXIT
Prior to your deperture you will be required to ot tain
clearence from the Trade Services Division, Economic and
Scientific Section, General Headquarters in Tokyo or Oseka
which will indicete that all personal expenses have been
met and thet your ratlon cards have been returned. Arrange-
ments for travel to the port of exit and boarding of your
outgoing vessel or aircreft may be made at your hotel.
You will clear through Japanese Customs and Immigra-
tion, now operating under the supervision of the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers, and surrender your identi-
fication card.
If you are In Japan longer than 60 days, you must have
an exit clearance before leaving. Application for such
exit clearance should be submitted to Trade Services Divi-
slon at least l0 deys prior to the anticlpated date of
departure to ensure no deley or inconvenience in departure.

many metropolitan areas of Japen during the war and conse-
quently there is a serious housing shortage. The Japanese
government's Roard of Trade has rehabilltated rulldings
for your accommodation in Tokyo, Nagoya, Osaka and Kyoto.

9

=== Page 291 === (confidence 0.99)
However, Japanese real estate acquired by any non-
Japanese or by a foreign-controlled firm for business pur-
poses, either by purchase or long-term lease, will require
validation by both SCAP and the Japanese government or the
transaction will be void. This requirement anplies not
only to commercial entrants but to acquisitions made by all
foreign nationals and firms owned or controlled by them.
SCAP and Japenese government validations are required
for the acquisition of property interests in the following
categories: stocks and shares; rights to a portion of the
profits, sales or output of a Japanese firm; patents of
Japanese origin and rights thereunder, and options or oth
agreements to acquire such interests.
Jepanese government validations will be freely deter-
mined by the Japanese government itself, vhich if it wishes
to permit an acquisition or lease must certify to its eco-
nomic desirability and to the absence of duress, fraud or
undue influence assignable in any way to the Occupation.
Permission to do business does not constitute the right
to any spectal allocation of materials. Similarly, foreign
businessmen must obey Japanese laws, particularly economic
control legislation.
Restitution. Seeking restitution of holdings in Japan
prior to the war, the restitution of which would contribute
to the economic rehabilitation of Japan.
Pre-war United Nations national property owners are
permitted to come to Japan to seek restitution of their
property, but the number of individuals in this category
is limited to those whose activitles will contribute to
the economic rehabilitation of Japan.
Upon arrival In Japan the owner, or duly cuthorized
agent, may make application to the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers, through the Civil Property Custodian,
through his government's Reparations and Restitution Mission
in Tokyo, for the return of his property. Regardless of
whether the property was sold or liquidated by the Japanese
Government during the war, owners are entitled to return of
their property. However, in such cases, the owner must
agree, as s conditlon of the return of his property, to
refund to the Japanese Government the amount of money re-
ceived as payment or that was deposited in a blocked account
in the owner's name at the time of sale or liquidation.
Repayment of such amounts, however, will only be due after
the settlement of any clai-s for loss or damage that the
owner may make against the Japanese Government. If the
property has been leased to a third party by the Japanese
custodlan or administrator, the owner will have the oppor-
tunity of terminating the lease and obtaining vacant pos-
session. If, however, the property has been requisitioned
by the Occupation Forces for their use, vacant possession
will not be obtained by the owner until the property is
released by the Occupation Forces.
10

=== Page 292 === (confidence 0.99)
In accepting the return of the property, the owner will
not be required to renounce any cleim he may have against
the Japanese Government or Its nationals for damages to the
property. Since the procedures for adjudicating or settling
claims ageinst the Japanese Government or Its nationals
have not yet been determined, it is mt possible to state
the extent or nature of compensation which may be provided
in respect to cleims for loss or damege suffered before re-
turn of the property, nor when such clalrs will be acted
upon. An owner who is unable or unwilling to assume control
of his property at the present time will not be compelled
to sccept its return, nor will any rights he may have be
prejudiced by his not resuming control at present. Until
the property 1s returned to the owner; the Japanese Govern-
ment has sole responsibility for its preservation and pro-
tection under the direction of the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers, but after its return its future maintenance
and eny rehabilitation costs are the responsibility of the
owner.

IV. STATUS F COMMERCIAL ENTRANTS.
7. A commercial entrant who is authorized to enter Japan under
the provisions of this circular is not a member of the occupation forces
or attached to or accompanying such forces. Such entrant is subject
not only to all laws and regulations specifically made applicable by
their terms to commercial entrants, but also to all laws and regulations
applicable in Japan to other nationals of his country who are not members
of, or attached to, or accompanying the occupation forces, whether such
laws or regulations are promulgated by the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers or pursuant to authority delegated by him, or by the
Japanese Government.
8. All commercial entrants in Japan are prohibited from committing
any act prejudicial to the security of the occupation farces or any
member thereof, or any persons attached to or accompanying the occupation
forces. Commercial entrants are subject to arrest by military authorities
for the violation of applicable regulations of the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers, including but not limited to the following prohibi-
tions:
a. Entering any of the following areas and installations:
(1) Temporary enclosures and stockades for war criminals.
(2) Dump areas containing confiscated war materials and
supplies.
5

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.99)
(Cir 1)
(3)
Military installat ons, except as necessary in the
transaction of authorized business.
(4)
All other areas and installations that have been
placed "off limits" for security purposes.
b. Carrying arms or other lethal weapons.
c. Failing to comply with travel re gulations pramulgated by
the occupation forces.
9. a. A commercial entrant is subject to arrest for offenses
comitted in Japan against the laws and regulations mentioned in para-
graph 7, or for violation of the provisions of tiis circular. Such
arrest in the case of a United Nations national will be made by the
military police, except that in areas where troops of the occupation
forces are not actually present on duty, and there is reasonable evi-
dence that a serious offense has been committed, or when otherwise
ordered by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Fowers or his authorized
subordinates, arrest may be made by the Japanese police. Arrest in the
case of persons not United Nations nationals may be made by either the
military or the Japanese police.
b. Trial for offenses committed in Japan, where the accused
is a United Mations national, will be by military court. In a case
where the accused is not a United Mations national, in the absence of
specific direction by competent authority that the trial be by military
court, trial may be either by a Japanese criminal court or by military
court.
10. All persons or organizations in Japan not attached to or accompa-
nying the occupation forces are subject to Japanese civil law and are
subject to the jurisdiction of Japanese courts in civil matters, except
as modified by applicable directives of the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers. The Japanese Govemment has been prohibited from im-
posing discriminatory taxes against non-Japanese nationals and from im-
posing any tax based upon, or measured in, non-yen currencies. United
Nations nationals are also exempt fran any tax against them or their
property designed primarily to meet reparations or other charges in-
curred by the Japanese Govemment because of World Nar II, such as the
Capital Levy, the Non-War Sufferers Special Tax, and part of the War
Indemmity Special Measures Law. Except as indicated above, yen income
is subject to Japanese taxation, and the Japanese Government has the
power to carry out property attachment proceedings and other law en-
forcement actions of a noncriminal nature. liowever, decisions in all
civil cases affecting United Mations nationals or organizations, or
in which such nationals or organizations are or may become parties,
shall be subject to review, including revision or such other action as
may be considered necessary by the Supreme Jommander for the Allied
Powers or his authorized representatives.
11. Commercial entrants will conduct their business and investment
activities, including international trade, in accordance with provisions
of Circular 2, General Headquarters, Suprene Commander for the Allied
Pomers, 4 January 1949, and applicable regulations. With the exception
of items for personal use and such comnercial items as may be necessary
and appropriate in the conduct of authorized business, (see paragraph
4e(2)), all international trade will be conducted subject to approval
6

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.99)
(Cir 1)
by the Japanese Board of Trade (Boeki Cho). Although negotiations with
individual Japanese are p rmitted, private contracts and agreements are
not valid unless approved and validated by the appropriate governmental
authority.
12. Commercial transactions with occupation force agencies or
personnel are not permitted without specific written authority.
13. Except as specifically pormitted by license or other written
authority, commercial entrants are not authorized to use or possess
military payment certificates.
1h. Financial transactions will be conducted in accordance with ex-
change controls and other applicable occupation force and Japanese Govern-
ment regulations. All foreim currencies, including United States dollars,
will be converted or deposited within 48 hours after arrival. Use of such
currencies in Japan is illegal except that during the first 4& hours after
arrival commercial entrants may purchase foreign trade payment certificates
(in amounts necessary to meet personal expenses) and Japanese yen. There-
after purchases of foreim trade payment certificates and yen may be made
with acceptable foreign exchange instruments. In all cases except in the
course of business activities spesifically permitted by the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers, conversion of foreign currency into yen
through other than authorized official sources and at official rates is
prohibited.

21. Automobiles may be brought to Japan only after a semipermanent

VII. RESPONSIBILITIES OF COMMANDING GENERAL, EIGHTH ARMY.
23. The Commanding General, Eighth Army, has the same responsibility
for entry and exit of commercial entrants as for other categories of in-
dividuals and will perform the following additional functions:
a. Receive commercial entrants at ports of entry and super-
vise necessary customs inspection to prevent the importation of items
prohibited by current directives.
b. Register commercial entrants immediately upon entrance.
c. Check military permit, passport, and authority to enter
against list furnished by the Suprems Commander for the Allied Powers.
d. Check medical records and provide necessary immmisation
and vaccination (requirements are the same as for occupation personnel).
e. Coordinate with the Japanese Government to arrange for the
prompt movement of the individual.
f. Provide emergancy attention. Except in case of emsrgency
or when enroute on occupation force trains, occupation force rations
and billets will not be provided.
g. Provide emergency medical treatment at military medical
facilities to the extent of availability and limited to that required
9

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.99)
(Cir 1)
to prevent undue suffering or to save life or limb. Elective medical
and dental care will not be provided. Medical services provided will
be on a reimbursement basis at the dollar rates prescribed in current
publications.
h. Keep necessary records and notify General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, whenever a commercial entrant
scheduled to depart from Japan fails to depart.
i. At port of exit, verify clearance issued by General Head-
quarters, Supreme Conmander for the Allied Powers, and collect identi-
fication cards.
VIII. MISCELIANECUS.
24. Japanese government commercial hotels are primarily established
and operated for the use of commercial entrants. Fermission to engage
in intemational trade, or other business or investment activities granted
by or in accordance with Circular 2, General Headquarters, Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers, 1h January 1949, does not entitle other personnel,
including non-Japanese who have been continuously residents in Japan since
2 September 1945 or who have been permitted entry by the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers for the purpose of establishing permanent residence,
to the use of Japanese government facilities operated primarily for com-
mercial entrants, or to the privileges and use of occupation facilities
herein authcrized for commercial entrants.
25. No member of the occupation forces and no person attached to or
accompanying the occupation forces, including his dependents may partici-
pate in international trade or other business or investment activity in
Japan for personal gain or advantage, or on behalf of any private person
or agency, except as specifically licensed by General Headquarters, Su-
preme Commander for the Allied Powers.
26. With the exceptions noted above commercial entrants may desig-
nate Japanase, Japanese firms, or foreign nationals in Japan as their
agents, subject to approval of the Japanese Government and the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers.
27. Comercial entrants may utilize to the extent of availability
the International Hospitals located at Tokyo, Yokohama, Kobe and Osaka.
Payment for services received will be made in Japanese yen.
28. Religious services available to the occupation forces will be
available to commercial entrants.
29. Amusemants: a. Concerts and similar entertainment and sports
contests for occupation forces where no admission charges are made will
be available to commercial entrants as spectators.
b. Entertainment where fees are charged to occupation forces
will be available according to contract agreements of the entertainment
concerned.

tend privileges involving payments will submit necessary applications
authorising them to accept and redeem foreign trade payment certificates.
