CIRCULAR )
14 January 1949
NO.....2)
FOREIGN BUSINESS AND INVESTMENT ACTIVITIES IN JAPAN
1. Rescission and Reference.
a. Rescission. Paragraph 3b, Circular 17, General
Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers,
2 June 1948.
b. Reference. Circular 1, General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, 14 January 1949.
2. Purpose. The purpose of this circular is to estab-
lish the conditlons under which non-Japanese nationals and
foreign controlled firms and their authorized agents may
conduct business and investment activity in Japan.
3. Definitions. a. A "foreign controlled firm," for
the purpose of this circular, is defined as a corporation,
institution, or other organization wholly owned or controlled,
directly or indirectly, by non-Japanese nationals.
b. "Business activity in Japan," for the purpose
of this circular, is defined as commercial or financial
transactions or industrial operations other than those con-
ducted solely for the occupation forces.
4. Entry. As indicated in referenced circular, lb
above, and under conditions set down therein, persons with
a trade, business, or investment interest in Japan who have
been sponsored by Allied or neutral governments are permitted
entry into Japan.
5. Permission to do business.
a. Military personnel and personnel attached to or
accompanying the occupation forces, including their depend-
ents, are prohibited from engaging in business or investment
activity in Japan, except as specifically licensed by General
Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers. Em-
ployment of dependents by private commercial concerns engaged
in international trade is authorized provided this employment
does not involve dependents serving on their own behalf or as
agents to make sales to or purchases from Japanese agencies
or individuals.
b. Non-Japanese nationals who have been in continu-
ous residence in Japen since 2 September 1945. or who have
beon permitted entry by the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers for the purpose of establishing permanent residence
Appendix II

=== Page 308 === (confidence 0.99)
(Cir 2)
in Japan, and firms wholly owned or controlled by them are
permitted to engage in business transactions in Japan on a
non-discriminatory basis with Japanese nationals, except as
provided in paragraph 8 below.
c. Effective the date of this circular, Allied and
neutral nationals or firms who are entitled to clain restora-
tion or restitution of properties or contract rights held
prior to 7 December l941 are permitted to resume their prewar
business activities in Japan on a non-discriminatory basis
with Japanese nationals, except as provided in paragraph 8
below.
d. In all other cases, effective the date of this
circular, non-Japanese nationals and foreign controlled firms
and their authorized agents are permitted to engage on a non-
discriminatory basis with Japanese na!ionals, except es pro-
vided in paragraph 8 below, in international trade and in
those specific business activities in Japan which positively
aid in Japanese economic rehabilitation, or provide a source
of foreign exchange for Japan or are otherwise in furtherance
of occupation objectives. Application to secure permigsion
for engaging in specific activities other than international
trade will be made to General Headcuarters, Supreme Commander
for the Allled Powers.
e. Notwithstanding the provisions of subparagraphs
a thru d above, persons and firms engaged in business involy-
ing the receipt of foreign exchange in Japan; the importation
or dissemination of educational, informational or cuitural
materials from abroad; or other activities which may hereafter
be designated, are required to be licensed by the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers. Persons and firms now op-
erating under license by the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers, which are not required to be licensed by this sub-
peragraph, may request termination of such licenses if they
so desire.
6. Compliance with Regulations of the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Fowers and Japanese Law. All permission to
engage in international trade and other business activity
granted by the above paragraph is contingent upon compliance
with regulations of the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers and Japanese Law, including applicable economic control
regulations. Violations thereof will constitute grounds for
termination of permisslon to do business and for deportation.
Laws particularly relevant in this connection are the Price
Control Ordinance, the Temporary Demand and Supply Adjustment
Law (governing allocations of criticel industrial materials)
and Japanese tax laws.
7. Critical shortages in the Japanese economy. At the
present time Japan is suffering from critical shortages of
materials, facilities and services. Business and industrial
operations conducted by non-Japanese nationals and firms may
be handicapped by such shortages end are hereby so informed.
The permission to do business granted by or pursuant to para-
graph 5 in no way constitutes grounds for eny special claim
on materials, facilities and services necessary to carry out
2

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.99)
(C1r 2)
the contemplated business operations or continue present
business operations. Non-Japanese nationals or firms wishing
to engage in business in Japan which would require Japanese
materials, facilities or services are advised to inquire of
the Japanese government Economic Stabilization Board as to
the prospective availability of such materials, facilities
and services and to guide themselves accordingly.
8. Acquisition of Properties and Rights. a. Effective
the date of this circular non-Japanese nationals and foreign
controlled firms permitted to do business by paragraph 5 above
and their authorized Japanese agents, are authorized to ac-
quire or lease properties and rights in Japan in accordance
with Japanese law, except that acquisition of property inter-
ests and rights in the following categories from Japanese
nationals, from firms in which Japanese nationals or firms
have a proprietary interest and from Japanese government
agencies, will be void unless validated by the Japanese Govern-
ment and the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers:
(1)
Acquisition of title to stocks and shares or
of an interest in the profits of an enter-
prise.
(2)
Acquisition of title to land and/or resi-
dence for busiress purposes, and to commer-
cial and industrial buildings and installa-
tions, and plant and facilitles attached
thereto. (Land and residences reasonably
required by an individual signatory to the
contract for his full or part time residence
are not business properties; all other land
and residences are business properties and
subject to the provisions of this subpara-
graph).
(3)
Leases for periods in excess of five years,
mortgages or other hypothecaticns, and ar-
rangements for or options to future acquisi-
tion of properties in the categories indi-
cated in subperagraphs (1) and (2) above.
(4)
Acquisition of patents of Japanese origin
and rights thereunder.
(5)
Acquisition of rights to a proportion of the
output or sales of an enterprise for a period
in excess of one year.
b. Applications for validation as required by sub-
paragraph a above will be submitted to the appropriate agency
of the Japanese government as designated.
c. Real properties acquired or leased by non-Japanese
nationals and foreign controlled firms are subject to requisi-
tion by the occupation forces on the same basis as Japanese
properties. Non-Japanese natlonals and foreign controlled
firms contemplating acquisition or lease of real properties
may secure information from General Headquarters, Supreme Com-
mander for the Allied Powers as to whether there is a foresee-
able occupation requirement for the properties involved.
3

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.99)
(C1r 2)
d. The requirements for validation established in
this paragraph shall not apply to restoration or restitution
of properties to prewar owners by the Japanese goverrment
under supervision of the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers nor shall anything in this paragraph be construed as
validating any acquisition or lease of properties or rights
which was made before the date of this circular and which
was contrary to existing laws or regulations of the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers or otherwise invalid at the
time made.
9. Remittances. Except as provided by special license
by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, conversion
of yen into foreign currency or exchange and agreements or
arrangements involving such conversion are prohibited.

proxies, instructions, or any other meens Intended to defeat
regulations of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
regarding foreign exchange or the conversion of foreign as-
sets; messages which grant or transfer translation, repro-
duction, performance or other rights concerning books, arti-
cles, plays, music, motion pictures, or other media of infor-
mation and expression; nor messages relating in any way to
copyrights except for description and explanation of the
authorized channels and procedures for handling such matters
and except for acknowledgment of rights arranged through the
authorized channels."
COMMERCIAL PAPERS: All commercial papers except the
following may be mailed: scores or sheets of music in manu-
script; manuscripts of works, or newspapers sent separately;
all papers of legal procedure and documents of all kinds
drawn up by ministerial officers. The malling of any of the
four of these categories of commercial papers is a violation
of regulations. CATALOGS, PHOTOGRAPES, DRAWINGS, PLANS, MAPS
AND PATIERNS may be mailed. SAMPLES AND SMALL PACKETS may
be mailed to and from Japan. The weight limit of packages
containing samples 1s 500 grams, (18 oz.). The weight limit
on packets of merchandise 1s 1 kilogram (2lbs., 3 oz.). Do
not confuse the mailing of samples and small packets with
the mailing of parcel post. Samples and small packets of
merchandise are not transmitted in the international mails
as parcel post. Procedures for shipment of samples, by ocean
freight or air express are available at the office of the
Board of Trade and the Foreign Trade and Commerce Division.
PRINTS: Mailing of books, newspepers and other periodi-
cal publications, u der the classification of "Prints", is
authorized from Japan only subject to the provisions and
limitations of the Universal Postal Union Convention.
PARCEL POST: Mailing is permitted by parcel post of
trade samples weighing more than 18 ounces and ordinary com-
mercial items of merchandise, to and from Japan, subject to
regulatory provisions shown below:
a. Existing regulations governing mailing of "Re-
lief" parcels, to Japan only, are not effected.
b. Weight limit will be in accordance with limita-
tions provided by Universal Postal Union Parcel Post Agree-
ment and/or applicable bilateral agreements, but not exceed-
ing 22 pounds per parcel.
c. Parcels originating in Japan which contain trade
samples will require licenses approved by the Board of Trade.
Appendix IV

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.99)
Customs Declaratlon tags indorsed "Trade Samples Only" must
be atteched. Parcels containing trade samples originating
elsewhere and destined for Japan will have attached thereto
Customs Declaration indicating the character of erticles
and the fact thst they are trade samples.
d. Parcels containing ordinary items of merchan-
dise in commercial quantities, to and from Japan, must be
accompanied by a certificate or photostatic copy of e li-
cense validated by the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers permitting such importation or exportation. The words
"Import License Inclosed" or "Export License Inclosed",
whichever is applicable, must te indorsed on the wrapper,
as well as the words, "This Package Mey be Opened for Cus-
toms Inspection".
e. All parcels entering Japan will be subject to
examination by Japanese Customs Service. All parcel post
packeges, samples and small packets entering Japan and sub-
mitted to customs examination are subject to a customs clear-
ance fee to be collected from the addressee upon delivery.
Importation of dutiable articles will be subject to prevall-
ing laws and regulations governing examination, appraisal
and assessment of duty (items in authorized quantities in
"relief" parcels will continue to be duty free).
f. Each parcel post package must have securely
attached thereto a "Customs Declaration" on which must be
shown full and precise details.
g. Neither insurance, registry nor C.O.D. service
1s included.
h. Undeliverable parcels (except "relief" parcels)
will be treated in accordence with the provisions of the
Universal Postal Union Parcel Post Agreement, or applicable
bilateral agreements.
i. Prohibited Articles:
(1) Items included under Article 16, Universal
Postal Union Parcel Post Agreement.
(2)
Articles prohitited in Jepan's Internation-
al regular-mails.
(3)
Any book, pamphlet, paper, writing, adver-
tisement, circular, printing or drawing
containing any matter advocating or urging
treason or insurrection against any Govern-
ment.
(4)
Articles prohitited under terms of 51-
laterel parcel post agreements between
Japan and other countries.
Jepan's authorized Internationel Air Mall Service will
embrace all mailable matter authorized now or later in the
international regular-mail service to and from Japan.

Mrs. Wiebke and her five year old son into Japan en route to Brasil
was communicated to the American Embassy, Nanking, by this Mission's

-2-
Mrs. Wiebke's clearance, as commuricated to Nanking and Dairen, was
made contingent upon Brazilian Government provision of funds for
her repatriation.
The Department's telegram under reference "a" above relayed
an incuiry from the Consulate in Dairen, concerning the status of
the viebke clearance. As this kission and interested sections of
Gi., CAP, had received no information concerning funds for
repatriation of the wiebke family, this Mission by telegram of
January 5, 1949, (reference "b" above) suggested to the American
Embassy, Nankin, that the transfer of funds be handled by authorise-
tion from the Brazilian Embassy in Nanking to the Portuguese
Diplomatic Agency, Tokyo, to uarantee expenses incident to travel
of Mrs. viebke and her son through Japan. The American Embasey,
Nanking, replied by telegram of January 26, 1949, (reference "c"
above) that the Brazilian mbassy in Nanking had stated that funds
to cover such expenses woulu be supplied through the Brasilian Embassy
in ashington and the Department of State. This Mission has had no
indication that the Brazilian Embassy has approached the Department
on the subfect.
It may be mentioned that there is no shipping scheduled at
present between airen and Japan, and it is therefore impossible to
say when Mrs. wiebke and her soi. will be able to proceed to Japan.
However, this Mission and GH, CAP consider it desirable to
complete clearance for transit through Japan of foreign residents
of airen who desire to leave that city, so that such individuals
can take advantage of any repatriation or other shipping which
may be aut orized by soviet authorities to enter Dairen on short
notice.
It is therefore reguested that this Mission be informed by the
most expeditious means, of receipt from the Brazilian Embassy in
Washington of funds or guarantees to cover expenses of the repatria-
tion of Mrs. Wiebke and her son to Brazil. It is also requested
that, if the Department deems it appropriate, the circumstances of
evacuation of foreign residents from Dairen be brought to the
attention of the Brazilian Embassy, and that the Embassy be
informally advised that in view of the small number of Japanese
reportedly remaining in Dairen it is improbable that more than one
additional repatriation vessel will proceed from Dairen to Japan.

UNCLASSIFIED
Department of State,
0MV-9
February 9, 1949
Washington, D. C.
Military Permits for members of Japanese
race.
Department's Airgram No. 228 of May 4, 1948.
The airgram under reference states Japanese
nationals returning to Japan may enter that country
without a military permit. The Embassy respectfully
inquires what procedure applies to members of the
Japanese race, who have become citizens of another
country by naturalization, or who may be dual nationals
by birth in a country other than Japan and who travel
with passports issued by the country of their birth.
In Mexico there are a number of Japanese who
have become naturalized Mexican citizens, as well
as Japanese born in Mexico who thereby have Mexican
nationality. Inquiries have been made at this
office as to the need for Military permits for
temporary visits to Japan by such persons.

=== Page 417 === (confidence 0.98)
De/R
FUTURERELEASE
PLEASE NOTE DATE
NATIONAL MILITARY ESTABLISHMENT
DEPARTMENT OF THE ARMY
Washington 25, D. C.
FOR E EASE AT 7:30 P.M. EST,
THURSDAY, FEBRURARY 10, 1949
RE 6700 Ext. 2528 - 71252
ARMY APPROVES 60-DAY VISITS
WITH RELATIVES IN JAPAN
A ocram authorizing individuals to enter Japan for a maximum period of
60 days for the purpose of visiting immediate relatives who are Japanese nationals
or whe are foreign nationals having permanent residence in Japan prior to Dec-
ember 1l, was announced today simultaneously by the Department of the Army
and by General of the Army Douglas MacArthur, Supreme Commander for the
Allied Iowers (SCAP). This program includes Japanese nationals, United States
nationals, and ell foreign nationals.
The lient provisions of this program are as follows:
1. Individuals entering Japan will be required either to bring with them
sufficient feod to fulfill their needs for the duration of their entire stay or to pur-
chase such foed from overseas supply stores in Japan.
2. The Japanese Government must certify that the host has adequate
housin (other than that required for use by occupation forces) and that the visit-
ors' entry will not inerease the critical housing shortage in Japan.
3. Subjeet individuals will travel by commerical transportation to Jap-
an and will not be authorized use of any occupation force facilities while in Japan.
4. Subject individuals must have legal access to dollar or pound ster-
ling credits since yen will be acquired only for dollar or pound sterling credits
and purch ses from overseas supply stores must be made with foreign trade pay-
ment certif:cates acquired with such credits. Visitors will be allowed to possess
only Japanese yen while in japan. All other currencies must be deposited upon ar-
rival in un aupropriate bank, and upon departure yen may net be converted into any
other currencies.
F. Vhile in Japan, visiters will be subject to all regulations promul-
gated by SCAF and Ci CFE and to Japanese law, and, in the event of any violation,
the individual will be sui ject to trial by Japanese courts or by the Cccupation
Forees Provost Sourt at the option of SCAP. SCAP reserves the right to termin-
ate the st of visitors for cause at any time.
1. In order to implement this program, an application form has been
devised which may be secured by prospective visiters in the country of their re-
sidence from those commerical carriers authorized to operate in Japan. This form
is to be filled out by the prospective visitor and returned to the commercial carr-
ier, who will then forward it to the Japanese Government for investigetion.
Finel approval for entry will be given by SCAP to the commerical carrier,
who will then notify the applicant of this approval. This notification to the appli-
cant by the commerical carrier will constitute authorization for application to the
Pacific Military Permit Branch, MDW, Room 2A-320, Pentagon Building, Washing-
ton s, D. C., for issuance of a Military Permit for Entry into Japan. This proced-
ure applies to United States citisens, Japanese nationals, and those foreign nation-
als who do not have their own representation or protecting power in Japan.
5:15 P.M.2/10/49
- END -

Gentlemen:
We enclose herewith application completed by Mr. Emmanuel Gran for permission
894.111/2-2849
to enter Japan. Mr. Gran is an architect and designer retained by this organi-
zation in that capacity, as consultant on our various construction and designing
problems.
The purpose of his visit to Tokyo is to consult with the staff of American Inter-
national Underwriters Corporation on local building problems which require the
benefit of his advice. Iir. Gran plans also to visit our affiliate offices in
Hong Kon and Manila in a similar capacity.
His entry to Japan depends upon receipt of the permit, which is anticipated to
be about the middle of March. As lir. Gran's application indicates, he is a
stateless person travelling under an Affidavit of Identity, in lieu of passport.
r. Gran maintains permanent residence in the United States and holds Re-entry
Permit #1537196, File #A7625113, valid to 2/18/50.
This company uarantees that Xr. Gran will be provided with funds for transportation
and that at no time will he become a public responsibility. Housing accommodations
will also be provided for Mr. Cran in Tokyo.
Mr. ran is presently in San Francisco and plans to continue from there, onward to
Hong Kong and Manila. Because of the uncertainty of his exact whereabouts on the

announced today simmltaneously by the Department of the Army and

seas supply stores in Japan.
"2. The Japanese Government must certify that the
host has adequate housing (other than that required for use by
occupation forces) and that the visitors' entry will not inersese
the critical housing shortage in Japan.

=== Page 461 === (confidence 0.97)

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: March 7, 1949.
SUBJECT:
Military permit to enter Japan for Mrs. Berezin, a French
national.
894.111/3-749
PARTICIPANTS: Miss Morgan, United Seamen's Service, N.Y.
Mrs. Mellor, DS.
d0
COPIES TO:
ero
1—1408
Miss Morgan of the United Seamen's Service, New York, called
Mrs. Mellor on the telephone for the purpose of finding out if the
United Seamen's Service could file an application to enter Japan
for three months on behalf of Mrs. Berezin, a French citizen.
Mr. Berezin is an American citizen. It developed in the course of
CS
the conversation that the Pacific Military Permit Branch had
returned Mrs. Berezin's application. I informed Miss Morgan that
A
SCAP was not granting permission at this time to wives to accompany
their husbands to Japan. Miss Morgan said Mr. Berezin works at their
various offices throughout the world and whenever it is possible
Mrs. Berezin goes along and works in the office also. She said
Mr. Berezin is at present at headquarters, which I assumed to be New
York. Mrs. Mellor informed Miss Morgan that SCAP had consistently
874.
refused permission to wives to accompany their husbands to Japan even
in some cases where the wives were specified as having an office in
the company.
Mrs. Mellor also informed Miss Morgan that in as much as
Mrs. Berezin is French and the French Government maintains a liaison
mission in Japan she should make her application through French
channels. She had evidently explored in that region because she
admitted having taken up the matter with the French Consulate General
in New York and it disclaimed any knowledge of such a thing. I
suggested that she get in touch with the French Embassy. Mr. Berezin
anticipates being in Washington tomorrow and Miss Morgan said he would
get in touch with me. I suggested to Miss Morgan that he call me on the
telephone and gave her my number in order to avoid wasting his time.
29768
CON:DS:LSMellor:mb

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: April 1, 1949.
Permission for Mr. Pablo Klein to enter Japan.
SUBJECT:
FW 894.111/3-3049
Mr. Julius Kahn, Secretary, Hermes Commercial Corporation,
PARTICIPANTS:
36 West 44th St., New York 18, New York.
Mrs. Mellor, DS.
COPIES TO:
GPO
1-1493
Mrs. Mellor called Mr. Julius Kahn to inform him that his Company's
representative, Mr. Pablo Klein had received permission to enter Japan
between May 1 and June 1, 1949 for a stay of sixty days after his date
of entry.
Mrs. Mellor inquired if Mr. Klein were still in Caracas, Venezuela
and when informed that he is she suggested that the Department could
request the Pacific Military Permit Branch to issue a military permit
document in Mr. Klein's name in as much as there was ample time for the
document to reach Mr. Klein by air mail. Mr. Kahn said if the Depart-
ment would obtain a military permit for Mr. Klein and send it in his
care he would forward it to Mr. Klein.
Mr. Klein expressed his thanks for the Department's call and for
the suggestion regarding Mr. Klein's Theater clearance.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

DIVISION OF PROTECTIVE SERVICES
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
April 5, 1949.
Mr. Yriart, Uruguayan Embassy, called on 'phone having been referred
to DS by RPA, and stated that a Uruguayan business man was in San
Francisco, having purchased a ticket at Montevideo from PANAM for Tokyo,
and he appears to be without documentation to enter Japan. Mr. Yriart
saidthe telegram seems to be furnishing additional information but the
Embassy has had nothing previous on the case. He wondered if PANAM
would sell a ticket to Tokyo to a person who was not documented to enter.
I informed him (on the first call) that Uruguay had a diplomatic
representative at Tokyo named Mr. Fleurquin and that the Consul at San
894
Francisco should telegraph the Uruguayan representative giving him the
necessary information to enable SCAP to issue a MP. He called again and
said the Uruguayan representative was withdrawn about four months ago.
He thought Sweden had represented Uruguay previous to that but not at
present. I informed him that the Department had never heard of the
withdrawal of the Uruguayan representative.
1/4-549
(PMPB-Army Dept.)
Mrs. Flickinger/ suggested that the State Department send a cable
to SCAP to find out if the Uruguayan representative has been withdrawn
and if so who is its Protecting Power. It has been drafted.
4/6/49 Mr. Yriart authorized that the cable be charged
to the Uruguayan Embassy
Jelegrome to CAP, Tolayo
